The mantrin, in the rite of secret mantra,
Always following the preceding rite,
Having taken up white powder,
With a mind that has become compassion,
Always keeping Acala in mind,
Should first lay down the colored powder.
Then the second colored powder,
Red, he should take up and lay down.
The third colored powder, yellow,
He should lay down with great certainty.
Then he lays down one like emerald,
Then lays down one like sapphire.
Whatever color is taught for any deity,
That is the very maṇḍala of that one.
So too the color of the deity:
For pacifying, enriching and fierce conduct,
Its secret mantra is held to be the same.
This is the teaching of the secret.
Whatever secret mantras of the Tathāgata there are,
They are proclaimed to be of Mahendra.
Those of Padmapāṇi are of water;
Those of Vajrin are of wind.
The messengers and the other servants,
And whatever belong to the class named the wrathful ones,
Who act everywhere without obstruction:
For all of them the maṇḍala is fire and wind.
Or else all of these
Possess the full force of efficacy.
Sated with the nectar of emptiness,
Their eyes of compassion are stainless;
Well clad in the armor of loving-kindness,
Endowed with the prowess of stainless joy,
They strive intently to draw out
The beings sunk in the ocean of existence.
They do not abide in saṃsāra,
Nor do they dwell in nirvāṇa;
Having settled on the very limit of reality,
They work for the benefit of sentient beings.

Then the Lord of Secrets, Vajrapāṇi, looking upon the maṇḍala of the assembly in its entirety, spoke these words:

What this Teacher, the Omniscient One free of evil,
The Blessed One, will explain
With certainty: the occasions
On which obstructors arise,
Listen to it, all you friends.
Obstructors are of many kinds:
Gods, asuras, yakṣas and humans.
Among these, obstructors are of two kinds:
Those whose sphere is outer and those whose sphere is inner.

Coughing, shortness of breath, fever and vomiting,
Pallor sickness and loss of appetite:
These and other such fevers,
Which arise in unbearable forms
From wind, cold, heat and the like,
Should be known as the inner obstructors.
The great outer obstructors
Are humans and likewise nonhumans.
These thorns to wisdom
Arise, through conduct of many various kinds,
In order to bring the world to ruin;
They bewilder those who abide in austerity.
Through the ritual of action that has been seen,
One should turn them back again.
Obstructors that come having become heat
Should be pacified by what is cooling.
The wise one, by means of the antidotes,
Should cure each of them in turn.
Or else, for all the obstructors,
Once the mantrin has firmly set the boundary,
Wisdom is declared to be supreme
In pacifying all obstructors.

Here ends the great maṇḍala blessing called The Arising of the Tathāgatas, from the noble Great Vairocana's Manifest Awakening, Emanation and Blessing, the king of Great Vehicle sūtras, the supreme of secrets, set out in sequence.

It was translated, edited and finalized by the Indian preceptor Śīlendrabodhi and the chief editor-translator, the monk Paltsek.

Translator's notes

This is the opening of the Mahāvairocanābhisaṃbodhi Tantra. The names of the vajra holders are translated by meaning (apart from Nārāyaṇa and Vajrapāṇi), since their Sanskrit forms cannot all be confirmed with confidence from the Tibetan alone. "Having blessed the day of the Tathāgata that transcends the three times" renders དེ་བཞིན་གཤེགས་པའི་ཉི་མ་དུས་གསུམ་ལས་འདས་པར་བྱིན་གྱིས་བརླབས་ནས; ཉི་མ can mean either "sun" or "day," and the Chinese version of this tantra reads "day." In "those to be tamed by a buddha," the written བདག་གིས་ ("by me") makes no sense here and is taken as a slip for བ་དག་གིས་, plural agent. Vajrapāṇi's opening sentence, beginning with ཅི་ལྟར, could also be read as a question ("how is it that the tathāgatas...?"); it closes as a statement and is rendered so. "Madder colored" renders ཁ་དོག་ལེ་བརྒན, the reddish-orange of madder dye. "Little difficulty" in the final paragraph follows ཚེགས་ཆུང་ངུས as earlier in the passage; the text here reads ཚེགས་ཆུང་དུས, which is taken as a slip for the same phrase. Several names in the list of gods and nāgas are identified with the help of the parallel Chinese version of this tantra and are less than certain: ནོར་སྦྱིན ("Dhanada," otherwise an epithet of Kubera, here listed separately from ལུས་ངན "Kubera"); སྣ་ཚོགས་འབྱུང ("Viśvakarman"); ནོར་ལྡན ("Vāsuki"); ལྷ་མོ་དཀའ་ཐུབ་ཟློག, "the goddess who turns back austerities," taken as Umā, whose name is traditionally explained as "do not [practice austerities]"; and རྟག་ཏུ, taken as the name "the Eternal One" (Sanātana). མྱ་ངན་འདས ("Nirvāṇa"), འཇིག་རྟེན་གཙོ ("Chief of the World") and གདེངས་ཀ་མང ("Many-Hooded") could not be identified with confidence and are translated by meaning. In the list of views of the self, ཚད་མ ("the measure") and འཛིན་པ་པོ ("a holder") are rendered literally; ཤེད་ལས་སྐྱེས་པ and ཤེད་བུ are the Sanskrit manuja and mānava. "On each of the days that bear that mark" refers to the observance days of the preceding fast. ཕྱག་ན་རྡོ་རྗེ་ཆེར་འབྱུང is taken as "Vajrapāṇi of noble (literally 'great') birth." Several of these definitions are terse and their sense is not certain. "The mind of a human" (thinks "for the sake of that very other," དེ་ཉིད་ཀྱི་དོན་དུ) is rendered literally; the Chinese version reads "thinking of benefiting others." "The mind of error" is listed as འཁྲུལ་པའི་སེམས but defined as ནོར་བའི་སེམས; both mean "error." "Satisfied even with little" reads ཆུང་དུས as ཆུང་ངུས. The order of the dog and cat differs between the list and the definitions, and is kept as the Tibetan has it. The Tibetan reads "practice ... forty" (bzhi bcus) before "what are the ten?"; this is most likely a slip for "practice ... by ten" (spyod pas bcus), and "ten" is translated. The doubling of one through five refers to the multiplication of the basic mental states into the one hundred and sixty worldly minds. "Ground of conduct through devoted interest" renders adhimukticaryābhūmi. "Conferred together with all the tathāgatas" reads the Tibetan "de bzhin gshegs pas thams cad" as "de bzhin gshegs pa thams cad"; the stray instrumental appears to be a slip. "Resolute" renders zung thub, whose exact sense here is not certain. The chapter title in the colophon renders sems kyi khyad par rim par phye ba. "Monastic quarters" renders gra gang, which is unclear as written; it is read as grwa khang. The line "In all phenomena there is nothing whatsoever" renders the obscure "yong med"; the sense of this and the following line ("merely partial," nyi tshe) is uncertain. "Self-arisen protectors" (rang mgon) most likely refers to pratyekabuddhas. Saṃkusumita renders me tog kun rgyas; the full form of this buddha's name is often given as Saṃkusumitarāja. "Its center is called the essence, and its circle is completeness" explains the two parts of the Tibetan word for maṇḍala, dkyil and 'khor, corresponding to Sanskrit maṇḍa ("essence") and la. "Tomorrow, when midday has passed" renders sang gi gdugs 'das nas, whose precise sense is somewhat uncertain. The Mother is presumably the Mother of the Buddhas, the Perfection of Wisdom. "The one of great might, holding a lotus" (mthu chen) is presumably Mahāsthāmaprāpta; the Tibetan gives only "great might," so it is translated rather than named. The cord-stretching directions are given as the Tibetan states them; some of them (such as stretching "toward the north" from the northeast while facing west) are terse and their exact geometry is not certain. "Vajra Point" (rdo rje rtse mo) is translated literally; the corresponding figure in the Chinese version is Vajrasūcī, but the Sanskrit underlying this Tibetan is not certain. "Krodhacandra, the Lord" renders khro zla gtso bo; the Chinese parallel names this figure Krodhacandratilaka. In the Trailokyavijaya verse, lag na rdo rje rtse bran rgyan is read as "foremost attendant (rtse bran) and ornament of Vajrapāṇi," which fits the following line about receiving his command; the phrase is unusual. Among the Pure Abode gods, "Garland of Light" and "Far-Renowned" are translated literally because their Sanskrit forms cannot be fixed with confidence. Of the three uṣṇīṣas to the right of Ūrṇā, the first two names, Greatly Arising and Upward Arising (likely Abhyudgata and Udgata or similar), are rendered literally for the same reason. The figure in the third section, here rendered literally as "the Giver of the Supreme Vajra," occupies the position and has the iconography (saffron color, five locks, youthful form, blue utpala with vajra) that the maṇḍala of this tantra assigns to Mañjuśrī; the Tibetan epithet is translated as it stands. The water pot renders gunde, taken as kuṇḍī or kuṇḍikā. "The Wondrous Hero" (dpa' bo ngo mtshar can) is translated literally because its Sanskrit form is uncertain; the Sanskrit forms of the other seven bodhisattvas in that list follow the standard names of this maṇḍala. The line "brgya lam na ni res 'ga' zhig / 'byung 'gyur yang ni mi 'byung ngo" is understood to mean that buddhas appear only rarely and in some ages not at all. The meaning of the assembly's final line ("dbang du gyur") is taken as gaining mastery through the question being asked. The explanation of "color" (tshon) rests on a wordplay in the Sanskrit original, where the word for color is related to the word for attachment or delight; the Tibetan conveys this by saying beings cling to it and that it is the flavor of the dharmadhātu. "O fearless one" takes 'jigs med as an address to Vajrapāṇi; it could also refer to the successive enclosures. In the verse on the bodhisattvas bound to one more birth, "the ground of the purpose" renders dgos pa'i sa literally; the Chinese version here speaks of the ground of faith and understanding, and the Tibetan wording is unusual. The last line of the eighth-ground verse ('jig rten 'jig rten dbang du 'dod) is difficult; it is taken to mean that the world regards such bodhisattvas as its masters. "The purification of karmic life" renders las tshe rnam par sbyang; the Chinese version reads this as the purification of karmic birth. In the syllable list, the glosses follow the Tibetan as it stands even where they differ from the Sanskrit wordplay preserved elsewhere: for ka the Tibetan has "intention" (dgongs pa), where a gloss on action (kārya) would be expected, and for gha "utter ruin" renders gcig tu 'phungs pa, where the Chinese has "compactness." Several food offerings are obscure in the Tibetan and are given in transcription: gorbö (gor bod), mathuśira, phena, aśokavarti, parpaṭa and hvak (hwags) are likely Indian names for particular sweets and cakes, and their exact identity is uncertain. "Pale cakes" renders khur skya, and "sugar candy" renders li kha ra. The drawing of the places of the fire "in triangular form" takes mdo ru in that sense, which is uncertain. The names of the four vajra holders are given in their attested Sanskrit forms (Niṣprapañcavihārin "Abiding without Elaboration", Gaganāmala "Stainless Space", Vimalanetra "Stainless Eye", Vicitrāmbara "Wearing Various Garments"). Of the four companion bodhisattvas, only Dhāraṇīśvararāja is rendered in Sanskrit; the other three are translated from their Tibetan meaning, as their Sanskrit forms could not be identified with confidence. The four emblems on the remaining petals are likewise translated by meaning. The golden probe is the eye-surgeon's instrument (śalākā) used to remove cataracts, the image underlying the verse that follows. The phrase rendered "the Tathāgata need no longer be greatly concerned" (literally "the Tathāgata will not need to think greatly") apparently means that where such initiates dwell, the Tathāgata's work is already being done. The phrase rendered "abiding in unobstructed understanding" could also be taken as "abiding in an unobstructed rite (kalpa)"; the Tibetan rtog pa admits both. The sentence beginning "there exist" has no expressed subject in the Tibetan; from context it refers to the protective mantras the Buddha is about to reveal. In the instructions for binding the wind, "the quarter of the wind" is the northwest, the direction of Vāyu. The Tibetan names the bodhisattva here as "Samanta-Avalokiteśvara" and his samādhi as "Samantāvalokita" (Looking Everywhere); the bodhisattva is given simply as Avalokiteśvara. In the sentence about deities abiding in their own maṇḍala places, the grammar does not state the subject explicitly; it has been taken as the deities, which fits the verse on deity colors that follows. The deity rendered "the Chief of the Wrathful Moon" (khro zla gtso bo) is translated by meaning, as its Sanskrit form could not be identified with confidence. Likewise "the Vajra Point" (rdo rje rtse mo) and "the Vajra Chain" (rdo rje lu gu rgyud, probably Vajraśṛṅkhalā) are given in English. "Attendants" renders phyag brnyan, the servant-messengers of Vajrapāṇi's retinue. The gatekeeper gdul dka' ba ("Hard to Tame") is given as Durdānta, which matches its mantra. Several deities in this list are given in English because their Sanskrit forms could not be identified with confidence: the Great Hero, the Lord of Waters, the Wondrous One, the Giver of Fearlessness to All Beings, the Purifier of All Lower Realms, the One Tormented by Compassion, the Pacifier of All Torment, the One Who Has Attained Dhāraṇī, the One with the Jewel Mudrā in His Hand, and the One of Firm Intention. "With a tranquil mind" renders des pas, which may be a slip for dad pas, "with faith". The places listed for the rite include "a shrine of a single liṅga," taking མཚན་གཅིག as Sanskrit ekaliṅga, as in the Indian lists of sites; it could also be read "for a single night." ཞི་གནས in that list is rendered "a peaceful place" rather than "calm abiding," since a location is meant; it may stand for a shrine of Śiva (śivālaya). "Life-force and exertion" renders སྲོག་དང་རྩོལ་བ, the two halves of prāṇāyāma, which the text then glosses as wind and mindfulness. The names of the two vidyā-mantras, Intoxicating and Swift as Thought, are translated from the Tibetan since their Sanskrit forms cannot be identified with confidence; the second may render manojava. "The lord of the desire realm" is probably Māra as ruler of the Paranirmitavaśavartin heaven, but the text does not name him. The door guardian གདུལ་དཀའ is translated by meaning, "Hard to Tame," since its Sanskrit form cannot be identified with confidence; it may correspond to Durdharṣa, who appears with Abhimukha among the gatekeepers in this tantra. ལིག་བུ་མིག is taken as eye ointment (añjana), a standard substance in siddhi rites, and གིའུ་ཝང as bezoar (gorocanā). In the prose about austerities, སྩོལ is read as རྩོལ, "to strive," which the sense requires. གཟི་ཅན is here rendered "a person of splendor," the object of the subjugation rite; the glossary's nāga-king reading Manasvin does not seem to fit, and the verse that follows suggests a ruler ("lord of men"). The verse itself is terse: the syllables A and PA appear to stand for the practitioner and the target, marked with a lotus and a conch. The Mahendra maṇḍala (དབང་ཆེན) is the square, yellow maṇḍala of the earth element, associated with the syllable A, as VA is with water and RA with fire in what follows. In the list of substances, དང་པ ("what is called clear") is obscure and may be a corrupt reading. ཁྱུ་མཆོག is normally "bull"; in a list of white substances it may refer to something from a bull or to choice crystal, so the reading is uncertain. "Here, at this heart of awakening" renders སྙིང་པོ་བྱང་ཆུབ, taken as a metrical inversion of བྱང་ཆུབ་སྙིང་པོ, the seat of awakening (bodhimaṇḍa). "Mahendra," on which the practitioner is seated, here denotes the earth maṇḍala (the square yellow seat associated with Indra), as elsewhere in this text. The syllables RA, VA, HA, A and KHAṂ correspond to fire, water, wind, earth and space respectively. Vajradhara at the end is Vajrapāṇi, the Lord of Secrets. The last two lines of the closing verse of the chapter on accomplishing siddhi are terse; they are taken to mean that those who have attained this wisdom abide throughout the world and teach it, though the subject is not explicit. "Gentle śrāvakas" renders ཉན་ཐོས་དེས་པ, taking དེས་པ as "gentle, disciplined." The lines on recitation are terse. "With its limbs left deficient" renders ཡན་ལག་ཉམས་སྦྱར་བར, taken as a warning against reciting without completing the prescribed limbs of the practice. "The count of secret mantra, three hundred thousand" is read as the standard number of recitations for the approach. "The Sage of golden light" renders གསེར་འོད་ཐུབ་པ as a descriptive epithet; it is not identified here with a specific named buddha. "Vajrapāṇi, adorned with pointed tips" renders the obscure རྡོ་རྗེ་རྩེ་བྲན་རྒྱན, apparently referring to the prongs of the vajra. "Bound to one more birth" renders སྐྱེ་བ་གཅིག་གིས་ཐོགས་པ (ekajātipratibaddha). The protectresses Fearless, Remover of All Fears, Hard to Tame and Vajra Tamer of the Untamed are given in English because their Sanskrit forms cannot be fixed with confidence. "The Bestower of the Sacred Vajra" renders རྡོ་རྗེ་དམ་པ་སྦྱིན་པ literally; the description (youthful, five locks of hair, saffron color, utpala bearing a vajra, seed syllable maṃ) is that of Mañjuśrī, who occupies this place in the maṇḍala. The "three dharmas" whose path one is kept from transgressing are not specified in this passage; commentaries on this tantra relate them variously (for example to the three vehicles or to body, speech and mind). The phrase "empowered by itself" in the vajra-mudrā verse renders an elliptical line that may also be read as "shown through the buddhas' own empowerment." In the fang mudrā, the Tibetan reads བསྟོད "praise," which makes no sense in a hand gesture; it is taken as གཏོད "point, direct," and rendered "hold them pointed." The name of the great protectress, rendered literally as "Unbearable," could not be securely matched to a Sanskrit form. The "specific knowledges" are the four pratisaṃvids of a buddha. The female attendants (ཕྱག་བརྙན་མ) are the servant goddesses of Mañjuśrī's retinue; no single Sanskrit name is given here, so the term is rendered descriptively. Several of the bodhisattva names here are given in their Sanskrit forms as known from the retinues of Sarvanīvaraṇaviṣkambhin and Kṣitigarbha in this tantra: Apāyajaha (ངན་སོང་སྤོང་བ), Maitryabhyudgata (བྱམས་པ་འབྱུང་བ), Karuṇāmṛdita (སྙིང་རྗེས་བརླན་པ), Acintyamati (བློ་གྲོས་བསམ་གྱིས་མི་ཁྱབ་པ), Ratnapāṇi, Viśuddhamati and Gaganāmala. The closing line "of ཕྱག་ན་རིན་ཆེན" after the five-pointed vajra mudrā is an abbreviation that, in context, refers to the One with the Jewel Mudrā in His Hand rather than to Ratnapāṇi. "Krodhacandra" renders ཁྲོ་ཟླ, the wrathful deity elsewhere called Krodhacandratilaka; "Vajra Point" (རྡོ་རྗེ་རྩེ་མོ) is given in English because its Sanskrit form (perhaps Vajrasūcī, as the mantra suggests) is not certain. The uṣṇīṣa deities and the gods of the Pure Abodes are given in their Sanskrit forms where these are standard for this tantra's maṇḍala (Sitātapatra, Jayoṣṇīṣa, Vijaya, Vikiraṇa, Tejorāśi, Mahodgata, Abhyudgata, Anantasvaraghoṣa, Samantakusuma, Raśmimālin). "Proclaimer of Sound" (སྒྲ་རྣམ་པར་སྒྲོགས་པ) and "the One Without a Frown" (མི་གཉེར་བ) are rendered literally because their Sanskrit forms could not be identified with confidence. Among the mātṛkās the Tibetan reads ཚངས་པ, "Brahmā," where the female Brāhmī might be expected; it is kept as written. Here བསྟོད is used in the sense "raise." In the final paragraph, "those who experience the fruits of action, lifespan and karmic maturation and of those who cause them to be experienced" renders a compressed Tibetan phrase (ཟ་བ་དང་ཟར་འཇུག་པ); the emanated bodies appear in the guise of beings of every kind so as to suit each being. The two verse-like lines about understanding by the intellect are set as verse following their seven-syllable meter. In the maṇḍala-on-the-body verse, the line read "then in the village (གྲོང) as far as the heart" makes no sense; it is translated as "above that" (སྟེང), the evident reading in the sequence feet-to-navel, then up to the heart. In the line "auspicious and praised by the worlds," the Tibetan writes སྔགས ("mantra"), which makes no sense here; it is read as བསྔགས ("praised"), as in the parallel closing line "praised by kings." The four kinds of disciple are given compactly in the Tibetan (one accomplished at once, one in two stages, one neither, and the one complete in all characteristics); the rendering follows that wording without expanding it. The verses on the inner and outer fire offerings are terse, and the precise referents of "three components, three places, and abides in three" are not spelled out in the passage itself; they are rendered literally. "The sixth fascicle" marks a division (bampo) of the Tibetan text. "The vidyā Bhogavatī" renders རིག་སྔགས་ལོངས་སྤྱོད་ཅན་མ, and "all the glorious ones" renders དཔལ་རྣམས; the precise identity of the latter group of goddesses is not certain. རྡོ་རྗེ་རྩེ is rendered literally as "Vajra Point," since its Sanskrit form cannot be fixed with confidence. In the tathāgata maṇḍala, "rays should be made, twofold" follows the wording as it stands (གཉིས་བྱ་བ), whose exact sense is unclear. The names of the Vajradharas in the opening list are given in English, since their Sanskrit forms cannot all be identified with confidence; the line division of some (for example "the Gentle One" as a separate name and "the Other Vajra") is uncertain. The Tibetan "rgyal dang rnam par rgyal ba" beside the Sun is taken as the goddesses Jayā and Vijayā, the Sun's attendants, distinct from the uṣṇīṣa deity Vijaya earlier. "gnyer ba'i lag pa," rendered "the hand of devotion," is uncertain. "skem byed" is taken as Skanda and "dka' zlog" as Umā. "The words of the twelve limbs" are presumably the twelve syllables placed on the disciple's body after the purifying burning; the text does not specify them here. The final line of the verse on the pledge ("dam tshig can ni gzhan mi bya") is terse; it is taken to mean that the pledge holder is to be regarded as no different from the Buddha. The "double dot" of the maṇḍala of the abiding Dharma renders klad kor gnyis, as in the preceding passage. The phrase "klad kor can snyems pa dang bcas pa," rendered "bearing the anusvāra, and majestic," is uncertain. "Bless oneself as the Vajrasattva of action" renders "bdag las kyi rdo rje sems dpar byin gyis brlabs"; it could also be read as a reference to Karmavajrasattva. The list of "thorns" corresponds to the standard list of wrong views of a self (ātman, sattva, jīva, poṣa, manuja, mānava, kāraka). "Eating food one has not taken up" renders "ma blangs pa'i zas," probably food that one has not solicited or accepted in the ordinary way; the precise ascetic practice is not specified. The line "In the waxing fortnight of the moon" has no expressed main verb in the Tibetan and is left as it stands. Two body places in the arrangement of letters are uncertain: "chu sor," rendered "the bladder," and "nam mtshong," tentatively rendered "the eyes" for the syllable ra. The armpits are given twice (for da and for u ū) as the Tibetan stands. "The Vajra Holder" (rdo rje can) and "Padmapāṇi" (phyag na padma) are taken as the heads of the vajra and lotus families. "Protected by a sign" renders mtshan ma; it likely refers to a woman marked as betrothed or under some other protective token, but the Tibetan does not specify. In the passage on idle chatter, "the bodhisattva's cause will continue without interruption" renders rgyu yang bar du gcod pa med pa; rgyu could also be read as "wandering," giving "his course through saṃsāra will be unbroken." The phrase "carries out what has been commanded" renders bka' grub par byed pa, describing the śrāvaka training as mere observance of precept. The five bases of training listed for the householder bodhisattva here are abstention from killing, stealing, sexual misconduct, lying, and wrong view; the text names wrong view where the usual list has intoxicants, and the translation follows the text. "Vajrapāṇi" directly after the mantra is taken as an address opening the verses that follow. "This ocean of world systems adorned with the essence of the flower base" renders the Tibetan descriptively; it most likely corresponds to the Kusumatalagarbhavyūhālaṃkāra world system familiar from the Avataṃsaka, but the Sanskrit form is not certain, so the name is translated. The letter linked to "existence cannot be observed" is written pa, the same letter as for ultimate truth just before it; in the order of the alphabet one would expect bha (for bhava, existence). The translation keeps the letter as written. Several statements, such as "free of suchness," "free of giving," and "free of peace," are rendered literally as the Tibetan gives them; each plays on the Sanskrit word beginning with the letter in question. "Multiplied tenfold" renders "the ten minds transformed into ten kinds," the arithmetic that yields the hundred syllables. In the verses on the lord of secret mantras, fire, water, wind, the sun, and the moon are named as elements and celestial bodies that he is not, so they are rendered as common nouns rather than as the deities Agni, Varuṇa, and Vāyu. The ten དབང here, beside the ten grounds, are the ten masteries (vaśitā) of a bodhisattva, not empowerments. The forty-four fires are given in this Tibetan with Indian names partly transliterated and partly translated; translated names that could not be identified with confidence (Brahmā's Food, Side-Guardian, Holder, Joiner, Treasure, Giver of Strength, Desire-Born, Messenger, Terrifying, World Holder, Stupefier, and others) are rendered by meaning, and transliterated ones (Śadvi, Avanika, Vicici, Havi, Mṛṭa, Dāruṇa) are given as the Tibetan spells them. The line about Side-Guardian and Atharvan is obscure, and the phrase rendered "russet in its coloring" (སྤུན་ལ) is uncertain. Brahmā's address "O Great Brahmā" is kept as the Tibetan has it. "Even if a buddha had made them" renders literally a line whose sense is that nothing can protect the obstructors from this wrathful practice. The "bindings" (བཅིང་བ) introduced by Vajrapāṇi are bodily postures and mudrās. The lunar mansions are identified from their standard Tibetan names. The vidyā queen named གནོད་མཛེས beside Māmakī could not be identified with confidence and is rendered by meaning as "Beautiful Destroyer"; the same Tibetan elsewhere renders the name Sumbha. The "Lotus Holder" is presumably Avalokiteśvara. The syllable ra is contemplated as removing dust (Sanskrit rajas). The deity or mantra རྡོ་རྗེ་དམ་པ་སྦྱིན could not be identified with confidence and is rendered by meaning as "the Supreme Vajra Giver." The substitute for sandalwood, པུ་ཤེལ་ཙེ, and the incense ཤི་བ་ས་ཀ are unidentified plant substances and are given as the Tibetan spells them. "The king of the wrathful ones" here is presumably Acala. The planets and lunar mansions are identified from their standard Tibetan names. The figure called "Mahāśrī, the Noble White Lady" may correspond to Mahāśvetā; the name is rendered as the Tibetan gives it. The syllable placed in the Mahendra (earth) maṇḍala is given in the text as ya, and is translated as written. The material of the alternative lamp vessel (khams pa) is uncertain; "clay" is a tentative rendering, and brass or bell metal is possible. The thickness measure of the kindling sticks (chang gang) is uncertain; "a thumb in thickness" is tentative. The implement "gundhe" is a transliterated Indic word that I cannot identify with confidence and is left as written. The deity name gtsug spyod is rendered literally as "Crown Conduct," since I cannot identify its Sanskrit form with confidence; "Vajra Hook" (rdo rje lcags kyu, perhaps Vajrāṅkuśa) is likewise given in English. The dye le brgan, used for the thread, is rendered generically as "crimson." "A majestic one" (gzi brjid can) probably refers to a powerful person such as a ruler. "With his own seal" renders rang gi rgyas, which is terse; it likely refers to the mantrin's own mudrā. The figure placed in the place of the Beautiful Destroyer, khro zla gtso bo, is rendered literally as "Chief Wrathful Moon," as I cannot identify its Sanskrit form with confidence. "Stake" for gsal shing is tentative. The second verse spoken by Vairocana is terse: it appears to speak of the dependent (paratantra) and perfected (pariniṣpanna) natures, the "two" being these, characterized by the eight consciousnesses; the rendering follows the Tibetan closely. The phrase 'dren ldan, describing the syllable a, and yi ge 'dren pa, "the leading letters," are rendered literally; they may refer to the vowels or to letters that carry the others. The verses at the opening follow the statement that Vajrapāṇi addressed the Blessed One, yet the prose after them addresses the Lord of Secrets; the speaker is therefore left as the text gives it, without quotation marks. The confession formula says "from this time onward until I reach the heart of awakening" before confessing past deeds; this is translated as it stands. "Free of fever" renders རིམས་ནད་མི་མངའ, probably reflecting Sanskrit nirjvara, a standard epithet of nirvāṇa and of the Buddha. The word rendered "saplings" (Tibetan gel pa) is uncertain; it appears to denote young trees or shoots. "Adorned with the array of the flower-ground essence" likely reflects Sanskrit kusumatalagarbhavyūhālaṃkāra, the name of the world system familiar from the Avataṃsaka literature. "The Invincible One" renders mi bzod pa, and "the great protectress, the Invincible" renders mi bzad srung ba chen mo; the underlying Sanskrit names of these protective deities could not be identified with certainty. The "Mother of the Buddhas" is a standard epithet of the Perfection of Wisdom sūtras. "Uragasāra" renders sbrul gyi snying po, literally "snake essence," the name of a precious sandalwood. The implement given to the female servants, written khad ga ri, is most likely the Sanskrit kaṭāri, a dagger; this identification is tentative. "The Blessed Lady" appears to refer to the Eye of Space just mentioned. "Discriminating knowledge" renders so so yang dag par rig pa (Skt. pratisaṃvid), the analytical knowledges of a buddha. The verb in the name of the laugh, bsgral ba, here has its ordinary sense of ferrying beings across the ocean of existence, not the wrathful-rite sense. In the verse "He fully comprehends the masteries," དབང is taken in the sense of the bodhisattva's masteries (vaśitā) attained on the ten grounds, not ritual empowerment; likewise རྒྱུད in "the mindstreams of all the world" means the mental continua (saṃtāna) of beings, not "tantra." The sentence on bodhisattvas not accomplishing a fixed establishment of their own mind is terse; it is rendered literally. In the final question, དབང་ཐོབ་པ is rendered "attaining mastery" (vaśitā) rather than "empowerment," as the context is mastery over all phenomena. The phrase "leaders of the herd" renders ཁྱུ་མཆོག (Skt. ṛṣabha, "bull"), an epithet of the buddhas. Before the sword mudrā the Tibetan reads གང་བའི་ཕྱག་རྒྱ ("full mudrā"); since the phrase refers back to the mudrā just described, it is taken as a slip for གསང་བའི་ཕྱག་རྒྱ, "the secret mudrā." In the sword verse, འཇིགས་ཚོགས is read as འཇིག་ཚོགས, the perishable collection (satkāya). དབང་ཐོབ་པ in "gain mastery over all phenomena" is rendered as mastery rather than empowerment. In the final sentence the Tibetan reads མེ་སྨྲའོ, taken as a slip for མི་སྨྲའོ, "I do not declare." "Person" renders ཤེད་ལས་སྐྱེས་པ (Skt. manuja). "Cutting off the root" reads རྩ་བ་ཚད་པ as རྩ་བ་ཆད་པ (ཆད "cut off"), the evident reading for an unclear ཚད. "Mahendra" here, as in earlier pieces, names the earth maṇḍala (the square yellow maṇḍala of Indra), and the elements water, wind and fire are the corresponding maṇḍalas assigned to each mantra family. "Vajrin" (རྡོ་རྗེ་ཅན, "the one possessing the vajra") denotes the Vajra family lord. ཨན་ད་རྙིལ is the Tibetan spelling of Sanskrit indranīla, sapphire. སྐྱ་རྦབ is an uncommon disease name; it is rendered here as "pallor sickness," a sickness marked by a pale, wasted complexion, but its exact identification is uncertain. "Ritual of action" renders ལས་ཀྱི་ཆོ་ག, the rite of ritual activities (karma), presumably the rites taught earlier in the tantra.
